========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I guess what finally got me to come in is… I keep avoiding things. My own workshop, mostly. Used to spend half my day out there, tinkering with whatever needed fixing—old radios, shelves, you know. My wife used to say I had more coffee cans full of screws than sense.

And family gatherings too, if I think my brother-in-law might be there. I end up making excuses or asking who’s coming, planning around it. It’s been going on long enough that I’m just tired of arranging my whole life so I don’t run into those reminders.

Yeah… I suppose anxious is part of it. It’s more like I start thinking ahead and trying to steer around it before I even get there. Like with the workshop—I used to practically live out there, had everything in coffee cans and old jars, drove my wife nuts—but now I’ll find reasons not to go in.

I don’t know that I can say it happened there, not in the workshop exactly. The workshop just… well, it’s full of stuff that makes my mind go places. Funny, I still remember where every jar of nails used to be, but I couldn’t tell you where I put my glasses half the time.

After all this time, I’m just tired of living around those reminders, you know?\n\nDoctor: Did something upsetting or traumatic happen to you in your workshop or at a family gathering?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it happened there, not in the workshop exactly. The workshop just… well, it’s full of stuff that makes my mind go places. People talk, and someone brings up old stories, and then I’m looking for a reason to leave before I’ve even sat down. So… was there one upsetting thing right there at a gathering? I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… sometimes. It’s not all day every day, but it’ll come out of nowhere. I’ll be shaving, or trying to work on a painting — I took that up after I wasn’t working anymore, though I’m not very good at getting the colors right — and then suddenly I see his hand again, holding that heavy tool.

It’s like a picture pops into my head before I can stop it. And when it does, my chest tightens up. I try to push it away, think about something else, but it doesn’t always listen.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally got me to come in is… I keep avoiding things. My own workshop, mostly. Used to spend half my day out there, tinkering with whatever needed fixing—old radios, shelves, you know. My wife used to say I had more coffee cans full of screws than sense.\n\nAnd family gatherings too, if I think my brother-in-law might be there. I end up making excuses or asking who’s coming, planning around it. It’s been going on long enough that I’m just tired of arranging my whole life so I don’t run into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about going to your workshop or attending family gatherings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose anxious is part of it. It’s more like I start thinking ahead and trying to steer around it before I even get there. Like with the workshop—I used to practically live out there, had everything in coffee cans and old jars, drove my wife nuts—but now I’ll find reasons not to go in.\n\nSame with family things. If I hear my brother-in-law might be there, I start making plans to skip it or ask too many questions about who’s coming. After all this time, I’m just tired of arranging everything so I don’t run into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I get uneasy, and sometimes I’ll work myself up thinking about things, but “peaking within minutes”… I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there with a stopwatch on it.\n\nIt’s more like I start going down a path in my head and then I’m thinking about ten other things—like whether I left tools out, or if someone’s coming over—and before I know it I’ve talked myself out of going. So… I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your workshop or family gatherings that you're trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s… it’s the reminders, I guess. The workshop used to just be my place, you know, where I’d go putter around and lose track of the afternoon. I had old coffee cans full of screws and bolts, probably still do, unless my wife finally tossed some of them.\n\nBut now I keep steering clear of it. And family get-togethers, if I know my brother-in-law might be there, I start figuring out how not to go. I’ll ask who’s coming, make excuses, all that. After all this time I’m just worn out from planning my life around not running into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happened in your workshop or at a family gathering that you're trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can pin it down as cleanly as that. It’s not like I walk in and say, “Oh, there’s the one thing.” It’s more… I know I keep avoiding my own workshop, and I know I start dodging family get-togethers if my brother-in-law might be there.\n\nAnd then I get to arranging everything around it—asking who’s coming, making excuses, staying out of the shop even though I used to spend half my day out there. After all this time, I’m just tired of living around those reminders, you know?\n\nDoctor: Did something upsetting or traumatic happen to you in your workshop or at a family gathering?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it happened there, not in the workshop exactly. The workshop just… well, it’s full of stuff that makes my mind go places. Funny, I still remember where every jar of nails used to be, but I couldn’t tell you where I put my glasses half the time.\n\nAnd the family gatherings, those get complicated. People talk, and someone brings up old stories, and then I’m looking for a reason to leave before I’ve even sat down. So… was there one upsetting thing right there at a gathering? I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I just know those places and people set something off in me, and I tend to avoid them.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid your workshop and family gatherings because you fear something bad will happen to you when you are there?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s like I’m afraid something bad is going to happen right then and there. It’s more that I keep steering clear of my workshop, and any family get-together where my brother-in-law might be, because of what it stirs up.\n\nI came in because I’m tired of it, honestly. Tired of checking who’s going to be there, making excuses, working around my own life just so I don’t run into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or thoughts that you can't get out of your head, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not all day every day, but it’ll come out of nowhere. I’ll be shaving, or trying to work on a painting — I took that up after I wasn’t working anymore, though I’m not very good at getting the colors right — and then suddenly I see his hand again, holding that heavy tool.\n\nIt’s like a picture pops into my head before I can stop it. And when it does, my chest tightens up. I try to push it away, think about something else, but it doesn’t always listen.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen involving your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Involving him, yes, in the sense that he’s tied up with what I can’t seem to shake, but saying it straight like that makes it sound cleaner than it feels.\n\nHe’s my wife’s side of the family, and you know how those things get—every holiday there’s seating and casseroles and someone’s always late. I start thinking about all that instead of answering you. I’m sorry. I guess I’m not sure how to explain it without making it into some big courtroom statement.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. The workshop just… well, it’s full of stuff that makes my mind go places. Funny, I still remember where every jar of nails used to be, but I couldn’t tell you where I put my glasses half the time.\n\nAnd the family gatherings, those get complicated. I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I just know those places and people set something off in me, and I tend to avoid them.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid your workshop and family gatherings because you fear something bad will happen to you when you are there?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s like I’m afraid something bad is going to happen right then and there. It’s more that I keep steering clear of my workshop, and any family get-together where my brother-in-law might be, because of what it stirs up.\n\nI came in because I’m tired of it, honestly. Tired of checking who’s going to be there, making excuses, working around my own life just so I don’t run into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or thoughts that you can't get out of your head, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not all day every day, but it’ll come out of nowhere. I’ll be shaving, or trying to work on a painting — I took that up after I wasn’t working anymore, though I’m not very good at getting the colors right — and then suddenly I see his hand again, holding that heavy tool.\n\nIt’s like a picture pops into my head before I can stop it. And when it does, my chest tightens up. I try to push it away, think about something else, but it doesn’t always listen.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen involving your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Involving him, yes, in the sense that he’s tied up with what I can’t seem to shake, but saying it straight like that makes it sound cleaner than it feels.\n\nHe’s my wife’s side of the family, and you know how those things get—every holiday there’s seating and casseroles and someone’s always late. I start thinking about all that instead of answering you. I’m sorry. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I suppose that’s the honest answer. I came in because I’ve been steering clear of my own workshop, of all places, and any family thing where he might show up.

It sounds foolish when I say it, because a man ought to be able to walk into his own workshop without making excuses. I used to spend hours out there, even if half the time I was just organizing screws into jars — my wife used to tease me about that. But now I plan around it, and around holidays or dinners too, just so I don’t have to run into him or anything that brings it all back.

After all this time, I’m tired of arranging my life around avoiding those reminders.

I… I’m having trouble saying the “what happened” part straight out. What I can say is it’s got me avoiding things I shouldn’t be avoiding.

I’ll be shaving, or trying to work on a painting — I took that up after I wasn’t working anymore, though I’m not very good at getting the colors right — and then suddenly I see his hand again, holding that heavy tool.\n\nIt’s like a picture pops into my head before I can stop it. And when it does, my chest tightens up. I try to push it away, think about something else, but it doesn’t always listen.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen involving your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d put it that way. I start thinking about all that instead of answering you. I’m sorry. I guess I’m not sure how to explain it without making it into some big courtroom statement.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you that involves your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that plainly. But I don’t want to say it wrong. My daughter always tells me I take too long getting to the point—she’s probably right. I just… I don’t know how to put it into a neat yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you involving your brother-in-law that you are trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s the honest answer. I came in because I’ve been steering clear of my own workshop, of all places, and any family thing where he might show up.\n\nIt sounds foolish when I say it, because a man ought to be able to walk into his own workshop without making excuses. It’s… him being tied to that workshop in my mind, I guess. I don’t want to get into all of it, but I came in because I keep staying away from my own workshop, and I avoid any family get-together where he might be there.

Which is ridiculous, really, because I used to practically live out there. Had coffee cans full of nails and little labels on things—though half the labels fell off, so I don’t know what good they did. But now I find excuses. Same with dinners or holidays.

So what I’m trying not to think about is whatever comes up when I think of him and that place. After all this time, I’m tired of planning my life around not running into those reminders.

After all this time, I’m just tired of living around those reminders, you know?\n\nDoctor: Did something upsetting or traumatic happen to you in your workshop or at a family gathering?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it happened there, not in the workshop exactly. The workshop just… well, it’s full of stuff that makes my mind go places. Funny, I still remember where every jar of nails used to be, but I couldn’t tell you where I put my glasses half the time.\n\nAnd the family gatherings, those get complicated. People talk, and someone brings up old stories, and then I’m looking for a reason to leave before I’ve even sat down. So… was there one upsetting thing right there at a gathering? I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I just know those places and people set something off in me, and I tend to avoid them.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid your workshop and family gatherings because you fear something bad will happen to you when you are there?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s like I’m afraid something bad is going to happen right then and there. It’s more that I keep steering clear of my workshop, and any family get-together where my brother-in-law might be, because of what it stirs up.\n\nI came in because I’m tired of it, honestly. Tired of checking who’s going to be there, making excuses, working around my own life just so I don’t run into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or thoughts that you can't get out of your head, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not all day every day, but it’ll come out of nowhere. I’ll be shaving, or trying to work on a painting — I took that up after I wasn’t working anymore, though I’m not very good at getting the colors right — and then suddenly I see his hand again, holding that heavy tool.\n\nIt’s like a picture pops into my head before I can stop it. And when it does, my chest tightens up. I try to push it away, think about something else, but it doesn’t always listen.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen involving your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Involving him, yes, in the sense that he’s tied up with what I can’t seem to shake, but saying it straight like that makes it sound cleaner than it feels.\n\nHe’s my wife’s side of the family, and you know how those things get—every holiday there’s seating and casseroles and someone’s always late. I start thinking about all that instead of answering you. I’m sorry. I guess I’m not sure how to explain it without making it into some big courtroom statement.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you that involves your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that plainly. It involves him in my head, yes, but when you say “something traumatic happened to you,” I get stuck on the wording.\n\nI know I avoid being around him, and the workshop sort of gets tangled up with it too. But I don’t want to say it wrong. My daughter always tells me I take too long getting to the point—she’s probably right. I just… I don’t know how to put it into a neat yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you involving your brother-in-law that you are trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s the honest answer. I came in because I’ve been steering clear of my own workshop, of all places, and any family thing where he might show up.\n\nIt sounds foolish when I say it, because a man ought to be able to walk into his own workshop without making excuses. I used to spend hours out there, even if half the time I was just organizing screws into jars — my wife used to tease me about that. But now I plan around it, and around holidays or dinners too, just so I don’t have to run into him or anything that brings it all back.\n\nAfter all this time, I’m tired of arranging my life around avoiding those reminders.\n\nDoctor: What happened involving your brother-in-law that you're trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: I… I’m having trouble saying the “what happened” part straight out. What I can say is it’s got me avoiding things I shouldn’t be avoiding.\n\nI keep steering clear of my own workshop, and that used to be my place, you know. I had every tool where I wanted it—well, mostly. My wife would say I spent more time moving things around than fixing anything. And family get-togethers too… if there’s a chance my brother-in-law will be there, I start finding reasons not to go.\n\nSo whatever you call it, I’m tired of planning my life around not running into him, or the place, or the reminders of it. It’s gone on too long.\n\nDoctor: What is it about your brother-in-law that you're trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: It’s… him being tied to that workshop in my mind, I guess. I don’t want to get into all of it, but I came in because I keep staying away from my own workshop, and I avoid any family get-together where he might be there.\n\nWhich is ridiculous, really, because I used to practically live out there. Had coffee cans full of nails and little labels on things—though half the labels fell off, so I don’t know what good they did. But now I find excuses. Same with dinners or holidays.\n\nSo what I’m trying not to think about is whatever comes up when I think of him and that place. After all this time, I’m tired of planning my life around not running into those reminders.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I know you’re asking whether it happened to me or someone else, but my mind kind of slips off the question.

It’s tied up with my brother-in-law and that workshop, that’s what I can say. I keep thinking if I say it one way, then it makes it more real, and if I say it another way… well, I don’t know. So… I don’t know. Or I’m not ready to say it that plainly yet.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally got me to come in is… I keep avoiding things. My own workshop, mostly. Used to spend half my day out there, tinkering with whatever needed fixing—old radios, shelves, you know. My wife used to say I had more coffee cans full of screws than sense.\n\nAnd family gatherings too, if I think my brother-in-law might be there. I end up making excuses or asking who’s coming, planning around it. It’s been going on long enough that I’m just tired of arranging my whole life so I don’t run into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about going to your workshop or attending family gatherings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose anxious is part of it. It’s more like I start thinking ahead and trying to steer around it before I even get there. Like with the workshop—I used to practically live out there, had everything in coffee cans and old jars, drove my wife nuts—but now I’ll find reasons not to go in.\n\nSame with family things. If I hear my brother-in-law might be there, I start making plans to skip it or ask too many questions about who’s coming. After all this time, I’m just tired of arranging everything so I don’t run into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I get uneasy, and sometimes I’ll work myself up thinking about things, but “peaking within minutes”… I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there with a stopwatch on it.\n\nIt’s more like I start going down a path in my head and then I’m thinking about ten other things—like whether I left tools out, or if someone’s coming over—and before I know it I’ve talked myself out of going. So… I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your workshop or family gatherings that you're trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s… it’s the reminders, I guess. The workshop used to just be my place, you know, where I’d go putter around and lose track of the afternoon. I had old coffee cans full of screws and bolts, probably still do, unless my wife finally tossed some of them.\n\nBut now I keep steering clear of it. And family get-togethers, if I know my brother-in-law might be there, I start figuring out how not to go. I’ll ask who’s coming, make excuses, all that. After all this time I’m just worn out from planning my life around not running into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happened in your workshop or at a family gathering that you're trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can pin it down as cleanly as that. It’s not like I walk in and say, “Oh, there’s the one thing.” It’s more… I know I keep avoiding my own workshop, and I know I start dodging family get-togethers if my brother-in-law might be there.\n\nAnd then I get to arranging everything around it—asking who’s coming, making excuses, staying out of the shop even though I used to spend half my day out there. After all this time, I’m just tired of living around those reminders, you know?\n\nDoctor: Did something upsetting or traumatic happen to you in your workshop or at a family gathering?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it happened there, not in the workshop exactly. The workshop just… well, it’s full of stuff that makes my mind go places. Funny, I still remember where every jar of nails used to be, but I couldn’t tell you where I put my glasses half the time.\n\nAnd the family gatherings, those get complicated. People talk, and someone brings up old stories, and then I’m looking for a reason to leave before I’ve even sat down. So… was there one upsetting thing right there at a gathering? I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I just know those places and people set something off in me, and I tend to avoid them.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid your workshop and family gatherings because you fear something bad will happen to you when you are there?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s like I’m afraid something bad is going to happen right then and there. It’s more that I keep steering clear of my workshop, and any family get-together where my brother-in-law might be, because of what it stirs up.\n\nI came in because I’m tired of it, honestly. Tired of checking who’s going to be there, making excuses, working around my own life just so I don’t run into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or thoughts that you can't get out of your head, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not all day every day, but it’ll come out of nowhere. I’ll be shaving, or trying to work on a painting — I took that up after I wasn’t working anymore, though I’m not very good at getting the colors right — and then suddenly I see his hand again, holding that heavy tool.\n\nIt’s like a picture pops into my head before I can stop it. And when it does, my chest tightens up. I try to push it away, think about something else, but it doesn’t always listen.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen involving your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Involving him, yes, in the sense that he’s tied up with what I can’t seem to shake, but saying it straight like that makes it sound cleaner than it feels.\n\nHe’s my wife’s side of the family, and you know how those things get—every holiday there’s seating and casseroles and someone’s always late. I start thinking about all that instead of answering you. I’m sorry. I guess I’m not sure how to explain it without making it into some big courtroom statement.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you that involves your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that plainly. It involves him in my head, yes, but when you say “something traumatic happened to you,” I get stuck on the wording.\n\nI know I avoid being around him, and the workshop sort of gets tangled up with it too. But I don’t want to say it wrong. My daughter always tells me I take too long getting to the point—she’s probably right. I just… I don’t know how to put it into a neat yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you involving your brother-in-law that you are trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s the honest answer. I came in because I’ve been steering clear of my own workshop, of all places, and any family thing where he might show up.\n\nIt sounds foolish when I say it, because a man ought to be able to walk into his own workshop without making excuses. I used to spend hours out there, even if half the time I was just organizing screws into jars — my wife used to tease me about that. But now I plan around it, and around holidays or dinners too, just so I don’t have to run into him or anything that brings it all back.\n\nAfter all this time, I’m tired of arranging my life around avoiding those reminders.\n\nDoctor: What happened involving your brother-in-law that you're trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: I… I’m having trouble saying the “what happened” part straight out. What I can say is it’s got me avoiding things I shouldn’t be avoiding.\n\nI keep steering clear of my own workshop, and that used to be my place, you know. I had every tool where I wanted it—well, mostly. My wife would say I spent more time moving things around than fixing anything. And family get-togethers too… if there’s a chance my brother-in-law will be there, I start finding reasons not to go.\n\nSo whatever you call it, I’m tired of planning my life around not running into him, or the place, or the reminders of it. It’s gone on too long.\n\nDoctor: What is it about your brother-in-law that you're trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: It’s… him being tied to that workshop in my mind, I guess. I don’t want to get into all of it, but I came in because I keep staying away from my own workshop, and I avoid any family get-together where he might be there.\n\nWhich is ridiculous, really, because I used to practically live out there. Had coffee cans full of nails and little labels on things—though half the labels fell off, so I don’t know what good they did. But now I find excuses. Same with dinners or holidays.\n\nSo what I’m trying not to think about is whatever comes up when I think of him and that place. After all this time, I’m tired of planning my life around not running into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Was the event involving your brother-in-law something that happened to someone else, or was it something that happened directly to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I know you’re asking whether it happened to me or someone else, but my mind kind of slips off the question.\n\nIt’s tied up with my brother-in-law and that workshop, that’s what I can say. I keep thinking if I say it one way, then it makes it more real, and if I say it another way… well, I don’t know. Funny thing, I can remember exactly where the old vise is on the bench, and the coffee can full of bent screws I never threw away, but I can’t seem to just give you a straight answer on this.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Or I’m not ready to say it that plainly yet.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I had every tool where I wanted it—well, mostly. My wife would say I spent more time moving things around than fixing anything. And family get-togethers too… if there’s a chance my brother-in-law will be there, I start finding reasons not to go.\n\nSo whatever you call it, I’m tired of planning my life around not running into him, or the place, or the reminders of it. It’s gone on too long.\n\nDoctor: What is it about your brother-in-law that you're trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: It’s… him being tied to that workshop in my mind, I guess. I don’t want to get into all of it, but I came in because I keep staying away from my own workshop, and I avoid any family get-together where he might be there.\n\nWhich is ridiculous, really, because I used to practically live out there. Had coffee cans full of nails and little labels on things—though half the labels fell off, so I don’t know what good they did. But now I find excuses. Same with dinners or holidays.\n\nSo what I’m trying not to think about is whatever comes up when I think of him and that place. After all this time, I’m tired of planning my life around not running into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Was the event involving your brother-in-law something that happened to someone else, or was it something that happened directly to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I know you’re asking whether it happened to me or someone else, but my mind kind of slips off the question.\n\nIt’s tied up with my brother-in-law and that workshop, that’s what I can say. I keep thinking if I say it one way, then it makes it more real, and if I say it another way… well, I don’t know. Funny thing, I can remember exactly where the old vise is on the bench, and the coffee can full of bent screws I never threw away, but I can’t seem to just give you a straight answer on this.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Or I’m not ready to say it that plainly yet.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid the event involving your brother-in-law happen directly to you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 76 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I can’t quite say yes or no to that. I know that sounds like I’m dodging, and maybe I am a little.

It’s like when I try to pin it down, my head goes somewhere else—like I start thinking about the old pegboard in the workshop, how I had the wrenches hanging by size, except the 9/16 was always missing. Funny what sticks, right?

But directly to me… I’m not sure I can answer that plainly right now. I know it involves him, and I know I’ve been avoiding that place and avoiding seeing him. That part I’m sure of.

My wife used to say I had more coffee cans full of screws than sense.\n\nAnd family gatherings too, if I think my brother-in-law might be there. I end up making excuses or asking who’s coming, planning around it. It’s been going on long enough that I’m just tired of arranging my whole life so I don’t run into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about going to your workshop or attending family gatherings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose anxious is part of it. It’s more like I start thinking ahead and trying to steer around it before I even get there. Like with the workshop—I used to practically live out there, had everything in coffee cans and old jars, drove my wife nuts—but now I’ll find reasons not to go in.\n\nSame with family things. If I hear my brother-in-law might be there, I start making plans to skip it or ask too many questions about who’s coming. After all this time, I’m just tired of arranging everything so I don’t run into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I get uneasy, and sometimes I’ll work myself up thinking about things, but “peaking within minutes”… I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there with a stopwatch on it.\n\nIt’s more like I start going down a path in my head and then I’m thinking about ten other things—like whether I left tools out, or if someone’s coming over—and before I know it I’ve talked myself out of going. So… I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your workshop or family gatherings that you're trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s… it’s the reminders, I guess. The workshop used to just be my place, you know, where I’d go putter around and lose track of the afternoon. I had old coffee cans full of screws and bolts, probably still do, unless my wife finally tossed some of them.\n\nBut now I keep steering clear of it. And family get-togethers, if I know my brother-in-law might be there, I start figuring out how not to go. I’ll ask who’s coming, make excuses, all that. After all this time I’m just worn out from planning my life around not running into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happened in your workshop or at a family gathering that you're trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can pin it down as cleanly as that. It’s not like I walk in and say, “Oh, there’s the one thing.” It’s more… I know I keep avoiding my own workshop, and I know I start dodging family get-togethers if my brother-in-law might be there.\n\nAnd then I get to arranging everything around it—asking who’s coming, making excuses, staying out of the shop even though I used to spend half my day out there. After all this time, I’m just tired of living around those reminders, you know?\n\nDoctor: Did something upsetting or traumatic happen to you in your workshop or at a family gathering?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it happened there, not in the workshop exactly. The workshop just… well, it’s full of stuff that makes my mind go places. I just know those places and people set something off in me, and I tend to avoid them.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid your workshop and family gatherings because you fear something bad will happen to you when you are there?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s like I’m afraid something bad is going to happen right then and there. It’s more that I keep steering clear of my workshop, and any family get-together where my brother-in-law might be, because of what it stirs up.\n\nI came in because I’m tired of it, honestly. Tired of checking who’s going to be there, making excuses, working around my own life just so I don’t run into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or thoughts that you can't get out of your head, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. And when it does, my chest tightens up. I try to push it away, think about something else, but it doesn’t always listen.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen involving your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d put it that way. I start thinking about all that instead of answering you. I’m sorry. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Posttraumatic Stress Disorder. You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S027\n  name: Sleep_Disturbance\n  description: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.\n  manifestation: Most nights I can get to bed, but I lie there listening for noises in the house or I wake up from a bad dream about the workshop and then I'm wide awake for an hour or two.\n- key: S069\n  name: Intrusive_Symptoms\n  description: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.\n  manifestation: Out of nowhere I'll see his hand with that heavy tool again, like a picture that pops into my head while I'm shaving or working on a painting, and it still makes my chest tighten.\n- key: S070\n  name: Trauma_Avoidance\n  description: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.\n  manifestation: I came in because I keep steering clear of my own workshop and any family get-together where my brother-in-law might be, and after all this time I'm tired of planning my life around not running into those reminders.\n- key: S071\n  name: Trauma_Related_Amnesia\n  description: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).\n  manifestation: I remember the shouting and the tool plain enough, but there's a blank spot right around how it actually ended, like somebody cut a few minutes out of the story.\n- key: S072\n  name: Negative_Beliefs\n  description: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.\n  manifestation: I used to think family was where you could let your guard down, but now I catch myself thinking you can't really trust people, even the ones who sit at your own table.\n- key: S073\n  name: Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame\n  description: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.\n  manifestation: I keep telling myself I should have put my foot down sooner and never let him stay with us, because maybe none of this would have happened if I'd handled it like a man.\n- key: S077\n  name: Irritability_Aggression\n  description: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: I'm not throwing things or anything, but I snap at my wife over little stuff, like where she put my paint brushes or if she asks me twice about supper plans.\n- key: S079\n  name: Hypervigilance\n  description: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.\n  manifestation: Even at the senior center or church basement, I find myself checking who came in, where the door is, and whether anyone is standing too close behind me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I still manage the basics, but I’ve stopped doing regular volunteer jobs that involve the workshop or big family events, and my painting comes in fits and starts because I avoid the place where I kept Had coffee cans full of nails and little labels on things—though half the labels fell off, so I don’t know what good they did. But now I find excuses. After all this time, I’m tired of planning my life around not running into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Was the event involving your brother-in-law something that happened to someone else, or was it something that happened directly to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I know you’re asking whether it happened to me or someone else, but my mind kind of slips off the question.\n\nIt’s tied up with my brother-in-law and that workshop, that’s what I can say. I keep thinking if I say it one way, then it makes it more real, and if I say it another way… well, I don’t know. Funny thing, I can remember exactly where the old vise is on the bench, and the coffee can full of bent screws I never threw away, but I can’t seem to just give you a straight answer on this.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I know that sounds like I’m dodging, and maybe I am a little.\n\nIt’s like when I try to pin it down, my head goes somewhere else—like I start thinking about the old pegboard in the workshop, how I had the wrenches hanging by size, except the 9/16 was always missing. Funny what sticks, right?\n\nBut directly to me… I’m not sure I can answer that plainly right now. I know it involves him, and I know I’ve been avoiding that place and avoiding seeing him. That part I’m sure of.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you experience or witness something traumatic involving your brother-in-law that directly affected you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I can put it that way. “Traumatic” is a big word, and “witness” and “experienced” makes it sound like I’ve got it filed in the right drawer, you know?

What I know is it involved him, and it affected me enough that I don’t want to be around him or go near that workshop. I start to answer you and then I’m thinking about the smell of sawdust and that old radio I kept on the shelf—only picked up one station half the time.

So… directly affected me, yes, in the sense that I can’t seem to leave it alone. But I’m not sure I can say more plain than that right now.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally got me to come in is… I keep avoiding things. My own workshop, mostly. Used to spend half my day out there, tinkering with whatever needed fixing—old radios, shelves, you know. My wife used to say I had more coffee cans full of screws than sense.\n\nAnd family gatherings too, if I think my brother-in-law might be there. I end up making excuses or asking who’s coming, planning around it. It’s been going on long enough that I’m just tired of arranging my whole life so I don’t run into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about going to your workshop or attending family gatherings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose anxious is part of it. It’s more like I start thinking ahead and trying to steer around it before I even get there. Like with the workshop—I used to practically live out there, had everything in coffee cans and old jars, drove my wife nuts—but now I’ll find reasons not to go in.\n\nSame with family things. If I hear my brother-in-law might be there, I start making plans to skip it or ask too many questions about who’s coming. After all this time, I’m just tired of arranging everything so I don’t run into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I get uneasy, and sometimes I’ll work myself up thinking about things, but “peaking within minutes”… I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there with a stopwatch on it.\n\nIt’s more like I start going down a path in my head and then I’m thinking about ten other things—like whether I left tools out, or if someone’s coming over—and before I know it I’ve talked myself out of going. So… I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your workshop or family gatherings that you're trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s… it’s the reminders, I guess. The workshop used to just be my place, you know, where I’d go putter around and lose track of the afternoon. I had old coffee cans full of screws and bolts, probably still do, unless my wife finally tossed some of them.\n\nBut now I keep steering clear of it. And family get-togethers, if I know my brother-in-law might be there, I start figuring out how not to go. I’ll ask who’s coming, make excuses, all that. After all this time I’m just worn out from planning my life around not running into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happened in your workshop or at a family gathering that you're trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can pin it down as cleanly as that. It’s not like I walk in and say, “Oh, there’s the one thing.” It’s more… I know I keep avoiding my own workshop, and I know I start dodging family get-togethers if my brother-in-law might be there.\n\nAnd then I get to arranging everything around it—asking who’s coming, making excuses, staying out of the shop even though I used to spend half my day out there. After all this time, I’m just tired of living around those reminders, you know?\n\nDoctor: Did something upsetting or traumatic happen to you in your workshop or at a family gathering?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it happened there, not in the workshop exactly. The workshop just… well, it’s full of stuff that makes my mind go places. Funny, I still remember where every jar of nails used to be, but I couldn’t tell you where I put my glasses half the time.\n\nAnd the family gatherings, those get complicated. People talk, and someone brings up old stories, and then I’m looking for a reason to leave before I’ve even sat down. So… was there one upsetting thing right there at a gathering? I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I just know those places and people set something off in me, and I tend to avoid them.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid your workshop and family gatherings because you fear something bad will happen to you when you are there?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s like I’m afraid something bad is going to happen right then and there. It’s more that I keep steering clear of my workshop, and any family get-together where my brother-in-law might be, because of what it stirs up.\n\nI came in because I’m tired of it, honestly. Tired of checking who’s going to be there, making excuses, working around my own life just so I don’t run into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or thoughts that you can't get out of your head, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not all day every day, but it’ll come out of nowhere. I’ll be shaving, or trying to work on a painting — I took that up after I wasn’t working anymore, though I’m not very good at getting the colors right — and then suddenly I see his hand again, holding that heavy tool.\n\nIt’s like a picture pops into my head before I can stop it. And when it does, my chest tightens up. I try to push it away, think about something else, but it doesn’t always listen.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen involving your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Involving him, yes, in the sense that he’s tied up with what I can’t seem to shake, but saying it straight like that makes it sound cleaner than it feels.\n\nHe’s my wife’s side of the family, and you know how those things get—every holiday there’s seating and casseroles and someone’s always late. I start thinking about all that instead of answering you. I’m sorry. I guess I’m not sure how to explain it without making it into some big courtroom statement.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you that involves your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that plainly. It involves him in my head, yes, but when you say “something traumatic happened to you,” I get stuck on the wording.\n\nI know I avoid being around him, and the workshop sort of gets tangled up with it too. But I don’t want to say it wrong. My daughter always tells me I take too long getting to the point—she’s probably right. I just… I don’t know how to put it into a neat yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you involving your brother-in-law that you are trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s the honest answer. I came in because I’ve been steering clear of my own workshop, of all places, and any family thing where he might show up.\n\nIt sounds foolish when I say it, because a man ought to be able to walk into his own workshop without making excuses. I used to spend hours out there, even if half the time I was just organizing screws into jars — my wife used to tease me about that. But now I plan around it, and around holidays or dinners too, just so I don’t have to run into him or anything that brings it all back.\n\nAfter all this time, I’m tired of arranging my life around avoiding those reminders.\n\nDoctor: What happened involving your brother-in-law that you're trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: I… I’m having trouble saying the “what happened” part straight out. What I can say is it’s got me avoiding things I shouldn’t be avoiding.\n\nI keep steering clear of my own workshop, and that used to be my place, you know. I had every tool where I wanted it—well, mostly. My wife would say I spent more time moving things around than fixing anything. And family get-togethers too… if there’s a chance my brother-in-law will be there, I start finding reasons not to go.\n\nSo whatever you call it, I’m tired of planning my life around not running into him, or the place, or the reminders of it. It’s gone on too long.\n\nDoctor: What is it about your brother-in-law that you're trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: It’s… him being tied to that workshop in my mind, I guess. I don’t want to get into all of it, but I came in because I keep staying away from my own workshop, and I avoid any family get-together where he might be there.\n\nWhich is ridiculous, really, because I used to practically live out there. Had coffee cans full of nails and little labels on things—though half the labels fell off, so I don’t know what good they did. But now I find excuses. Same with dinners or holidays.\n\nSo what I’m trying not to think about is whatever comes up when I think of him and that place. After all this time, I’m tired of planning my life around not running into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Was the event involving your brother-in-law something that happened to someone else, or was it something that happened directly to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I know you’re asking whether it happened to me or someone else, but my mind kind of slips off the question.\n\nIt’s tied up with my brother-in-law and that workshop, that’s what I can say. I keep thinking if I say it one way, then it makes it more real, and if I say it another way… well, I don’t know. Funny thing, I can remember exactly where the old vise is on the bench, and the coffee can full of bent screws I never threw away, but I can’t seem to just give you a straight answer on this.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Or I’m not ready to say it that plainly yet.\n\nDoctor: Did the event involving your brother-in-law happen directly to you?\n\nPatient: I… I can’t quite say yes or no to that. I know that sounds like I’m dodging, and maybe I am a little.\n\nIt’s like when I try to pin it down, my head goes somewhere else—like I start thinking about the old pegboard in the workshop, how I had the wrenches hanging by size, except the 9/16 was always missing. That part I’m sure of.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience or witness something traumatic involving your brother-in-law that directly affected you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can put it that way. “Traumatic” is a big word, and “witness” and “experienced” makes it sound like I’ve got it filed in the right drawer, you know?\n\nWhat I know is it involved him, and it affected me enough that I don’t want to be around him or go near that workshop. I used to spend hours out there, even if half the time I was just organizing screws into jars — my wife used to tease me about that. But now I plan around it, and around holidays or dinners too, just so I don’t have to run into him or anything that brings it all back.\n\nAfter all this time, I’m tired of arranging my life around avoiding those reminders.\n\nDoctor: What happened involving your brother-in-law that you're trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: I… I’m having trouble saying the “what happened” part straight out. What I can say is it’s got me avoiding things I shouldn’t be avoiding.\n\nI keep steering clear of my own workshop, and that used to be my place, you know. I had every tool where I wanted it—well, mostly. My wife would say I spent more time moving things around than fixing anything. And family get-togethers too… if there’s a chance my brother-in-law will be there, I start finding reasons not to go.\n\nSo whatever you call it, I’m tired of planning my life around not running into him, or the place, or the reminders of it. It’s gone on too long.\n\nDoctor: What is it about your brother-in-law that you're trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: It’s… him being tied to that workshop in my mind, I guess. I don’t want to get into all of it, but I came in because I keep staying away from my own workshop, and I avoid any family get-together where he might be there.\n\nWhich is ridiculous, really, because I used to practically live out there. Had coffee cans full of nails and little labels on things—though half the labels fell off, so I don’t know what good they did. But now I find excuses. Same with dinners or holidays.\n\nSo what I’m trying not to think about is whatever comes up when I think of him and that place. After all this time, I’m tired of planning my life around not running into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Was the event involving your brother-in-law something that happened to someone else, or was it something that happened directly to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I know you’re asking whether it happened to me or someone else, but my mind kind of slips off the question.\n\nIt’s tied up with my brother-in-law and that workshop, that’s what I can say. I keep thinking if I say it one way, then it makes it more real, and if I say it another way… well, I don’t know. Funny thing, I can remember exactly where the old vise is on the bench, and the coffee can full of bent screws I never threw away, but I can’t seem to just give you a straight answer on this.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Or I’m not ready to say it that plainly yet.\n\nDoctor: Did the event involving your brother-in-law happen directly to you?\n\nPatient: I… I can’t quite say yes or no to that. Funny what sticks, right?\n\nBut directly to me… I’m not sure I can answer that plainly right now. I know it involves him, and I know I’ve been avoiding that place and avoiding seeing him. That part I’m sure of.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience or witness something traumatic involving your brother-in-law that directly affected you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can put it that way. “Traumatic” is a big word, and “witness” and “experienced” makes it sound like I’ve got it filed in the right drawer, you know?\n\nWhat I know is it involved him, and it affected me enough that I don’t want to be around him or go near that workshop. I start to answer you and then I’m thinking about the smell of sawdust and that old radio I kept on the shelf—only picked up one station half the time.\n\nSo… directly affected me, yes, in the sense that I can’t seem to leave it alone. But I’m not sure I can say more plain than that right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I… I don’t know. I’m sorry, that sounds like I’m not listening, but I am. “See or hear it happen” is where I get stuck.

After all this time I’m just worn out from planning my life around not running into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happened in your workshop or at a family gathering that you're trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can pin it down as cleanly as that. It’s not like I walk in and say, “Oh, there’s the one thing.” It’s more… I know I keep avoiding my own workshop, and I know I start dodging family get-togethers if my brother-in-law might be there.\n\nAnd then I get to arranging everything around it—asking who’s coming, making excuses, staying out of the shop even though I used to spend half my day out there. After all this time, I’m just tired of living around those reminders, you know?\n\nDoctor: Did something upsetting or traumatic happen to you in your workshop or at a family gathering?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it happened there, not in the workshop exactly. The workshop just… well, it’s full of stuff that makes my mind go places. Funny, I still remember where every jar of nails used to be, but I couldn’t tell you where I put my glasses half the time.\n\nAnd the family gatherings, those get complicated. People talk, and someone brings up old stories, and then I’m looking for a reason to leave before I’ve even sat down. So… was there one upsetting thing right there at a gathering? I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I just know those places and people set something off in me, and I tend to avoid them.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid your workshop and family gatherings because you fear something bad will happen to you when you are there?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s like I’m afraid something bad is going to happen right then and there. It’s more that I keep steering clear of my workshop, and any family get-together where my brother-in-law might be, because of what it stirs up.\n\nI came in because I’m tired of it, honestly. Tired of checking who’s going to be there, making excuses, working around my own life just so I don’t run into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or thoughts that you can't get out of your head, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not all day every day, but it’ll come out of nowhere. I’ll be shaving, or trying to work on a painting — I took that up after I wasn’t working anymore, though I’m not very good at getting the colors right — and then suddenly I see his hand again, holding that heavy tool.\n\nIt’s like a picture pops into my head before I can stop it. And when it does, my chest tightens up. I try to push it away, think about something else, but it doesn’t always listen.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen involving your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Involving him, yes, in the sense that he’s tied up with what I can’t seem to shake, but saying it straight like that makes it sound cleaner than it feels.\n\nHe’s my wife’s side of the family, and you know how those things get—every holiday there’s seating and casseroles and someone’s always late. I start thinking about all that instead of answering you. I’m sorry. I guess I’m not sure how to explain it without making it into some big courtroom statement.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you that involves your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that plainly. It involves him in my head, yes, but when you say “something traumatic happened to you,” I get stuck on the wording.\n\nI know I avoid being around him, and the workshop sort of gets tangled up with it too. But I don’t want to say it wrong. My daughter always tells me I take too long getting to the point—she’s probably right. I just… I don’t know how to put it into a neat yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you involving your brother-in-law that you are trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s the honest answer. I came in because I’ve been steering clear of my own workshop, of all places, and any family thing where he might show up.\n\nIt sounds foolish when I say it, because a man ought to be able to walk into his own workshop without making excuses. I used to spend hours out there, even if half the time I was just organizing screws into jars — my wife used to tease me about that. But now I plan around it, and around holidays or dinners too, just so I don’t have to run into him or anything that brings it all back.\n\nAfter all this time, I’m tired of arranging my life around avoiding those reminders.\n\nDoctor: What happened involving your brother-in-law that you're trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: I… I’m having trouble saying the “what happened” part straight out. What I can say is it’s got me avoiding things I shouldn’t be avoiding.\n\nI keep steering clear of my own workshop, and that used to be my place, you know. I had every tool where I wanted it—well, mostly. My wife would say I spent more time moving things around than fixing anything. And family get-togethers too… if there’s a chance my brother-in-law will be there, I start finding reasons not to go.\n\nSo whatever you call it, I’m tired of planning my life around not running into him, or the place, or the reminders of it. It’s gone on too long.\n\nDoctor: What is it about your brother-in-law that you're trying to avoid remembering or thinking about?\n\nPatient: It’s… him being tied to that workshop in my mind, I guess. I don’t want to get into all of it, but I came in because I keep staying away from my own workshop, and I avoid any family get-together where he might be there.\n\nWhich is ridiculous, really, because I used to practically live out there. Had coffee cans full of nails and little labels on things—though half the labels fell off, so I don’t know what good they did. But now I find excuses. Same with dinners or holidays.\n\nSo what I’m trying not to think about is whatever comes up when I think of him and that place. After all this time, I’m tired of planning my life around not running into those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Was the event involving your brother-in-law something that happened to someone else, or was it something that happened directly to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I know you’re asking whether it happened to me or someone else, but my mind kind of slips off the question.\n\nIt’s tied up with my brother-in-law and that workshop, that’s what I can say. I keep thinking if I say it one way, then it makes it more real, and if I say it another way… well, I don’t know. Funny thing, I can remember exactly where the old vise is on the bench, and the coffee can full of bent screws I never threw away, but I can’t seem to just give you a straight answer on this.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Or I’m not ready to say it that plainly yet.\n\nDoctor: Did the event involving your brother-in-law happen directly to you?\n\nPatient: I… I can’t quite say yes or no to that. I know that sounds like I’m dodging, and maybe I am a little.\n\nIt’s like when I try to pin it down, my head goes somewhere else—like I start thinking about the old pegboard in the workshop, how I had the wrenches hanging by size, except the 9/16 was always missing. Funny what sticks, right?\n\nBut directly to me… I’m not sure I can answer that plainly right now. I know it involves him, and I know I’ve been avoiding that place and avoiding seeing him. That part I’m sure of.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience or witness something traumatic involving your brother-in-law that directly affected you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can put it that way. “Traumatic” is a big word, and “witness” and “experienced” makes it sound like I’ve got it filed in the right drawer, you know?\n\nWhat I know is it involved him, and it affected me enough that I don’t want to be around him or go near that workshop. I start to answer you and then I’m thinking about the smell of sawdust and that old radio I kept on the shelf—only picked up one station half the time.\n\nSo… directly affected me, yes, in the sense that I can’t seem to leave it alone. But I’m not sure I can say more plain than that right now.\n\nDoctor: Did you directly experience the traumatic event involving your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I can say that straight. Every time you ask it that directly, I sort of… go sideways in my head.\n\nI know it has to do with my brother-in-law, and I know that much isn’t nothing. But “directly experience” feels like one of those forms at the courthouse where there’s only two boxes and neither one fits right. I remember little things around it instead, like the dust on the concrete floor and how the light came through that one cracked window in the workshop.\n\nSo… I don’t know that I can give you a yes. I’m not saying no either. I just don’t have it clear enough, or I don’t know how to say it without getting all turned around.\n\nDoctor: Did you personally see or hear the traumatic event involving your brother-in-law happen?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I’m sorry, that sounds like I’m not listening, but I am. “See or hear it happen” is where I get stuck.\n\nI remember pieces around it, like the workshop and the way sound carries in there—metal tools, that little echo off the walls. My brother-in-law had this habit of clearing his throat before he said anything, and now I’m thinking about that instead of answering you.\n\nSo I can’t honestly tell you yes, I saw it, or yes, I heard it. I just don’t have that clear in my head right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I can’t say that I did. Not in a clean way, anyhow.

When you say “witness,” I picture standing there and seeing it plain, like when I was a kid and watched my father fix the mower in the driveway—everything out in the open, no confusion. This isn’t like that. With my brother-in-law, I get bits and edges, and then my mind wanders off to the workshop, the shelves, the tools, the way the place smelled.

So… I don’t know. I don’t want to say yes if I can’t really hold onto it.
